It is not simply:
\end{verbatim}

screen row screen column

\begin{verbatim}

because the document may be larger than the display.

A better representation:
\end{verbatim}

cursor:

line column

\begin{verbatim}

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Document:

Line 100

Cursor:

line = 100 column = 25

\begin{verbatim}

The renderer later decides where that position appears on the screen.

---

# Screen cursor versus document cursor

These are different.

Document position:
\end{verbatim}

line 100 column 25

\begin{verbatim}

Screen position:
\end{verbatim}

row 12 column 25

\begin{verbatim}

because the viewport may start at:
\end{verbatim}

line 88

\begin{verbatim}

The relationship is:
\end{verbatim}

screen\_row = document\_line - viewport\_start

\begin{verbatim}

Keeping these concepts separate prevents many bugs.

---

# Viewport

The viewport stores what portion of the document is visible.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

viewport:

top\_line = 50 left\_column = 0

\begin{verbatim}

The renderer uses this:
\end{verbatim}

Document:

50 51 52 53 54

Screen:

line 50 line 51 line 52 line 53

\begin{verbatim}

Scrolling changes the viewport.

It does not modify the document.

---

# Document

The document is the actual text being edited.

At this stage, do not worry about the final storage implementation.

Later chapters will discuss:

- simple line arrays
- gap buffers
- piece tables

The editor should interact with the document through operations:
\end{verbatim}

insert character

delete character

insert line

delete line

retrieve line

\begin{verbatim}

The storage method remains hidden.

---

# Selection

A selection represents a range of text.

Examples:
\end{verbatim}

select characters

select line

select block

\begin{verbatim}

Even if REMEDIT version one does not support visual selection, the architecture should leave room for it.

A selection might contain:
\end{verbatim}

start:

line column

end:

line column

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Mode

Editors often have different operating modes.

Examples:

Simple editor:
\end{verbatim}

NORMAL

\begin{verbatim}

and:
\end{verbatim}

INSERT

\begin{verbatim}

A command key can mean different things depending on the current mode.

For example:

In insert mode:
\end{verbatim}

a

\begin{verbatim}

means:
\end{verbatim}

insert character

\begin{verbatim}

In command mode:
\end{verbatim}

a

\begin{verbatim}

might mean:
\end{verbatim}

append text

\begin{verbatim}

The editor state must know which interpretation applies.

---

# The REMEDIT state model

A simple initial structure:
\end{verbatim}

EditorState

\{ Document document;

\begin{verbatim}
Cursor cursor;

Viewport viewport;

Mode mode;

Selection selection;

FileInfo file;
\end{verbatim}

\}

\begin{verbatim}

The exact implementation in assembly will differ, but the concept remains.

---

# The complete editor loop

Putting everything together:
\end{verbatim}

while running:

\begin{verbatim}
event = keyboard_read()

command = interpret(event)

modify(document, command)

update_viewport()

render()
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

Each stage has a responsibility.

---

# Why this architecture matters for REM

REM is a new computer architecture.

The temptation will be to optimise immediately:

- clever screen handling
- compressed data structures
- complex rendering

Avoid that initially.

The first goal is not the fastest editor.

The first goal is a correct editor whose parts can be understood.

A well-designed editor also becomes a demonstration platform.

Once REMEDIT exists, it can become:

- an assembly development environment
- a debugger front end
- a system monitor
- a test platform for new REM features

The editor is not just an application.

It is the first major piece of REM software infrastructure.

# Chapter 10 — Text Storage

The editor architecture introduced an important separation:
\end{verbatim}

Document

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Renderer

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Terminal

\begin{verbatim}

The renderer does not care how text is stored.

The keyboard does not care how text is stored.

The command system does not care how text is stored.

Only the document layer owns that decision.

This is important because text storage is one of the places where editor design becomes a trade-off.

A text file looks simple:
\end{verbatim}

Line one Line two Line three

\begin{verbatim}

but editing creates difficult operations:

- insert a character in the middle of a line
- delete characters
- insert new lines
- delete lines
- move large sections of text
- support undo
- handle large files

The storage method determines how easy those operations are.

---

# 10.1 The naive approach — single buffer

The simplest possible design is a single block of memory containing the entire file.

Example:

File:
\end{verbatim}

Hello World

\begin{verbatim}

Stored as:
\end{verbatim}

H e l l o {[}newline{]} W o r l d {[}newline{]}

\begin{verbatim}

Conceptually:
\end{verbatim}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 0\tabcolsep) * \real{0.6389}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
H e l l o {[}newline{]} W o r l d {[}newline{]} \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{verbatim}

The cursor is simply an offset:
\end{verbatim}

position = 8

\begin{verbatim}

This is attractive because it is easy to understand.

For a first test program, it is excellent.

---

# Inserting text

Suppose the file contains:
\end{verbatim}

Hello World

\begin{verbatim}

The user inserts:
\end{verbatim}

Beautiful

\begin{verbatim}

The buffer currently contains:
\end{verbatim}

Hello World

\begin{verbatim}

The insertion point is:
\end{verbatim}

Hello \textbar World

\begin{verbatim}

The buffer must become:
\end{verbatim}

Hello Beautiful World

\begin{verbatim}

That requires moving all data after the cursor:
\end{verbatim}

Before:

Hello \textbar World

After moving:

Hello \textbar{} World

Insert:

Hello Beautiful World

\begin{verbatim}

For a small file, this is fine.

For a large file, repeated editing becomes expensive.

---

# Problems with a single buffer

## Problem 1 — Insertions are expensive

Every insertion may require moving a large amount of memory.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

File size: 100 MB

Cursor position: beginning of file

\begin{verbatim}

Insert one character.

The editor may need to move almost:
\end{verbatim}

100 MB

\begin{verbatim}

of data.

---

## Problem 2 — Deletions have the same problem

Deleting a character:
\end{verbatim}

Hello World \^{} delete

\begin{verbatim}

requires shifting everything after it.

---

## Problem 3 — Undo becomes complicated

Undo requires knowing:

- what changed
- where it changed
- what data was removed

The storage model does not naturally preserve history.

---

## Problem 4 — Large files

A simple buffer assumes:

"The whole file fits comfortably in memory."

Modern systems usually have enough memory, but the design still limits scalability.

---

# Why start with it anyway?

Because it is simple.

The purpose of a first implementation is not to win editor performance benchmarks.

The purpose is to prove:

- keyboard handling works
- commands work
- rendering works
- files can be loaded and saved

A simple buffer removes complexity.

That is often the correct engineering decision.

---

# 10.2 Line-based storage

A more natural editor representation is a collection of lines.

Instead of:
\end{verbatim}

H e l l o {[}newline{]} W o r l d

\begin{verbatim}

store:
\end{verbatim}

Line 1:

H e l l o

Line 2:

W o r l d

\begin{verbatim}

Conceptually:
\end{verbatim}

Document

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 0\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1944}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Line 1 \\
Line 2 \\
Line 3 \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{verbatim}

This matches how humans think about text.

---

# Advantages of line storage

## Natural cursor handling

The editor already thinks in:
\end{verbatim}

line column

\begin{verbatim}

A line-based structure matches this directly.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Cursor:

line = 20 column = 5

\begin{verbatim}

The document can directly locate:
\end{verbatim}

Line 20 Character 5

\begin{verbatim}

---

## Simple rendering

The renderer needs:
\end{verbatim}

visible lines

\begin{verbatim}

A line-based document provides exactly that.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Viewport:

line 100 line 101 line 102 line 103

\begin{verbatim}

The renderer requests:
\end{verbatim}

give me lines 100-103

\begin{verbatim}

---

## Easy insertion of lines

Pressing Enter:

Before:
\end{verbatim}

Hello World

\begin{verbatim}

After:
\end{verbatim}

Hello

World

\begin{verbatim}

A line-based structure naturally supports:
\end{verbatim}

split line insert new line

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Problems with line storage

It is not perfect.

A line may still be stored as a contiguous array.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Line:

Hello World

\begin{verbatim}

Insert:
\end{verbatim}

Beautiful

\begin{verbatim}

Still requires moving characters inside that line.

---

Large documents also require managing:

- thousands of lines
- memory allocation
- line indexing

However, for a first editor, line storage is a very reasonable choice.

---

# 10.3 Gap buffers

A gap buffer is a classic editor data structure.

Many traditional editors use some variation of this idea.

The concept is simple:

Keep an empty space where the cursor is.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Before:

Hello World

\begin{verbatim}

Memory:
\end{verbatim}

H e l l o \_ W o r l d \^{} gap

\begin{verbatim}

The underscore represents unused space.

The cursor sits in the gap.

---

# Inserting characters

Insert:
\end{verbatim}

Beautiful

\begin{verbatim}

The editor fills the gap:
\end{verbatim}

H e l l o B e a u t i f u l \_ W o r l d

\begin{verbatim}

No large movement is required.

The next insertion is also cheap.

---

# Moving the cursor

The problem appears when the user moves far away.

Example:

Current gap:
\end{verbatim}

Hello \textbar World

\begin{verbatim}

User moves to:
\end{verbatim}

Hello World\textbar{}

\begin{verbatim}

The gap must move.

The editor shifts characters across the gap:
\end{verbatim}

Before:

Hello \_\_\_\_\_ World

After:

Hello World \_\_\_\_\_

\begin{verbatim}

Moving the cursor has a cost.

---

# Advantages of gap buffers

## Fast typing

This is the important case.

Most editing involves:

- typing characters
- small cursor movements

The gap is already where typing happens.

---

## Simple implementation

Compared with more advanced structures, a gap buffer is straightforward.

It requires:

- memory block
- gap position
- gap size

---

## Good for assembly

This is important for REMEDIT.

A complicated data structure creates more assembly work.

A gap buffer has relatively simple operations:
\end{verbatim}

move gap

insert character

delete character

\begin{verbatim}

---

# 10.4 Piece tables

Piece tables are a different approach.

They became popular in modern editors.

The idea:

Never modify the original file.

Instead:

- keep the original text
- store changes separately
- maintain a table describing the current document

---

# Example

Original file:
\end{verbatim}

Hello World

\begin{verbatim}

Stored permanently:
\end{verbatim}

Original buffer:

Hello World

\begin{verbatim}

User inserts:
\end{verbatim}

Beautiful

\begin{verbatim}

The inserted text goes into an add buffer:
\end{verbatim}

Add buffer:

Beautiful

\begin{verbatim}

The document becomes a list of pieces:
\end{verbatim}

Original:

Hello

Add:

Beautiful

Original:

World

\begin{verbatim}

The file is represented by references.

---

# Advantages of piece tables

## Excellent undo support

Because the original data is never destroyed, undo becomes easier.

The editor can simply modify the piece list.

---

## Efficient large files

Large sections of text do not need copying.

The editor moves references instead.

---

## Natural history

The structure already represents changes.

This fits well with:

- undo
- redo
- version history

---

# Problems with piece tables

They are more complicated.

The programmer must manage:

- piece records
- buffers
- indexing
- merging pieces

For a first assembly editor, this complexity may not be justified.

---

# 10.5 Choosing REMEDIT version 1

The purpose of REMEDIT version 1 is not to compete with mature editors.

The purpose is to create:

- a usable REM development tool
- a learning platform
- a foundation for future tools

The first implementation should favour:

- simplicity
- visibility
- debugging ability

over:

- maximum performance
- handling gigabyte files
- advanced undo systems

---

# Recommended progression

A sensible development path:
\end{verbatim}

Version 1

Simple line-based storage

\begin{verbatim}
    |

    v
\end{verbatim}

Version 2

Gap buffer for active editing

\begin{verbatim}
    |

    v
\end{verbatim}

Version 3

Piece table or advanced structure

\begin{verbatim}

Each stage teaches something.

---

# Why not start with the "best" design?

Because every additional feature creates another place to fail.

A piece table requires confidence in:

- memory management
- indexing
- rendering
- undo design

before you have even proved:

"Can I edit a file?"

---

# Recommended REMEDIT v1 structure

For the first working editor:
\end{verbatim}

Document

\begin{verbatim}
array of lines
\end{verbatim}

Line

\begin{verbatim}
length

character buffer
\end{verbatim}

Cursor

\begin{verbatim}
line

column
\end{verbatim}

Viewport

\begin{verbatim}
first visible line

first visible column
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

Operations:
\end{verbatim}

insert character

delete character

split line

join lines

load file

save file

\begin{verbatim}

This is enough to create a real editor.

---

# The engineering rule

The best data structure is not always the most advanced one.

The best first data structure is the one that lets you prove the rest of the system.

A working simple editor teaches more than an unfinished sophisticated editor.

Build the foundation.

Optimise after the machine works.

# Chapter 11 — Editing Operations

The previous chapter discussed how text is stored.

This chapter deals with what the user actually does with that text.

An editor is not complicated because it displays characters.

Displaying characters is the easy part.

The difficult part is maintaining a consistent document while the user constantly changes it.

Every editing operation must update several things:

- the document
- the cursor position
- the viewport
- the undo history
- the display

A useful rule:

> The document changes first. The screen changes afterwards.

The terminal is only a representation of the current state.

---

# 11.1 Insert character

The most common editor operation is inserting a character.

The user presses:
\end{verbatim}

a

\begin{verbatim}

The editor receives:
\end{verbatim}

CHARACTER(`a')

\begin{verbatim}

The editor must decide:

Where does this character belong?

The answer comes from the cursor.

Example:

Document:
\end{verbatim}

Hello World

\begin{verbatim}

Cursor:
\end{verbatim}

Hello \textbar World

\begin{verbatim}

Insert:
\end{verbatim}

X

\begin{verbatim}

New document:
\end{verbatim}

Hello XWorld

\begin{verbatim}

The operation is:
\end{verbatim}

insert character at cursor position

\begin{verbatim}

---

# The insert algorithm

For a line-based document:

Before:
\end{verbatim}

Line:

H e l l o W o r l d

\begin{verbatim}
    ^
    cursor
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

The editor:

1. Finds the current line.
2. Finds the cursor column.
3. Makes space for one character.
4. Copies existing characters after the cursor.
5. Inserts the new character.
6. Moves the cursor forward.

Conceptually:
\end{verbatim}

Before:

HelloWorld

After moving data:

Hello\_ \_World

Insert:

HelloXWorld

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Data structure considerations

The cost depends on the storage model.

## Simple array

A character insertion requires shifting data.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Insert near beginning:

A B C D E F

\begin{verbatim}
|
\end{verbatim}

Move:

B C D E F

\begin{verbatim}

Cost:
\end{verbatim}

O(n)

\begin{verbatim}

where `n` is the amount of text after the insertion point.

---

## Gap buffer

A gap buffer makes insertion efficient.

The cursor sits at the gap:
\end{verbatim}

Hello\_\_\_\_\_World \^{} cursor

\begin{verbatim}

Insert:
\end{verbatim}

X

\begin{verbatim}

becomes:
\end{verbatim}

HelloX\_\_\_\_World

\begin{verbatim}

No large movement is required.

---

# Cursor movement after insertion

Most editors move the cursor after inserting.

Example:

Before:
\end{verbatim}

Hello\textbar{}

\begin{verbatim}

Insert:
\end{verbatim}

X

\begin{verbatim}

After:
\end{verbatim}

HelloX\textbar{}

\begin{verbatim}

The cursor position changes:
\end{verbatim}

column = column + 1

\begin{verbatim}

This seems obvious, but every operation needs clearly defined cursor behaviour.

---

# 11.2 Delete character

Deletion is the opposite operation.

There are two common delete operations:

- Backspace
- Delete key

They appear similar but behave differently.

---

# Backspace

Backspace removes the character before the cursor.

Example:

Before:
\end{verbatim}

Hello World \^{} cursor

\begin{verbatim}

Press backspace:
\end{verbatim}

Hell World \^{} cursor

\begin{verbatim}

The cursor moves left.

---

# Delete key

Delete removes the character under the cursor.

Example:

Before:
\end{verbatim}

Hello World \^{} cursor

\begin{verbatim}

Delete:
\end{verbatim}

HelloWorld \^{} cursor

\begin{verbatim}

The cursor does not move.

---

# Delete algorithm

For a simple line buffer:

Before:
\end{verbatim}

H e l l o \^{} delete

\begin{verbatim}

Remove the character:
\end{verbatim}

H e l l \_

\begin{verbatim}

Move remaining characters left:
\end{verbatim}

H e l l

\begin{verbatim}

Update:

- line length
- cursor position
- undo history

---

# Edge cases

Deletion becomes interesting at boundaries.

## Beginning of line

Example:
\end{verbatim}

\textbar Hello

\begin{verbatim}

Backspace cannot remove a character.

Possible behaviours:

- do nothing
- join with previous line

Most editors choose joining.

---

## End of line

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Hello\textbar{} World

\begin{verbatim}

Backspace at the beginning of the next line:
\end{verbatim}

Hello \textbar World

\begin{verbatim}

may become:
\end{verbatim}

HelloWorld

\begin{verbatim}

This operation is called:
\end{verbatim}

join line

\begin{verbatim}

---

# 11.3 Split line

The Enter key is not just inserting a character.

A newline changes the document structure.

Before:
\end{verbatim}

Hello World\textbar{}

\begin{verbatim}

Press Enter:
\end{verbatim}

Hello World \textbar{}

\begin{verbatim}

The document now contains two lines.

---

# Split algorithm

Given:
\end{verbatim}

Line:

Hello World

Cursor:

Hello \textbar World

\begin{verbatim}

Create two lines:

First:
\end{verbatim}

Hello

\begin{verbatim}

Second:
\end{verbatim}

World

\begin{verbatim}

The cursor moves:
\end{verbatim}

World\textbar{}

The operation is:
\end{verbatim}

split current line at cursor

\begin{verbatim}

# Why split is important

Almost every text editor operation eventually depends on this.

Examples:

- pressing Enter
- pasting multiline text
- formatting code
- inserting files

The line structure changes.

---

# Memory considerations

For a line-based editor:

Before:
\end{verbatim}

Line array:

{[}Line 1{]} {[}Line 2{]} {[}Line 3{]}

\begin{verbatim}

Insert a new line:
\end{verbatim}

{[}Line 1{]} {[}New Line{]} {[}Line 2{]} {[}Line 3{]}

\begin{verbatim}

The line table must expand.

Possible implementations:

Simple:
\end{verbatim}

copy remaining line pointers

\begin{verbatim}

More advanced:
\end{verbatim}

linked list dynamic array

\begin{verbatim}

For REMEDIT version 1:

A dynamic array is probably sufficient.

---

# 11.4 Join lines

Joining is the reverse of splitting.

Before:
\end{verbatim}

Hello World

Cursor:
\end{verbatim}

\textbar World

\end{verbatim}

HelloWorld

\begin{verbatim}

The two lines become one.

---

# Join algorithm

1. Locate current line.
2. Locate previous line.
3. Append current line contents.
4. Delete current line.
5. Move cursor to join point.

Example:

Before:
\end{verbatim}

Line 1:

Hello

Line 2:

World

\begin{verbatim}

After:
\end{verbatim}

HelloWorld

\begin{verbatim}

Should a space be inserted?

Different editors behave differently.

Possibilities:

No space:
\end{verbatim}

HelloWorld

Space:

\end{verbatim}

HelloWorld

The editor design must choose.

The important thing is consistency.

Cursor movement seems simple:

LEFT RIGHT UP DOWN

but it creates many design decisions.

Example:

\end{verbatim}

Hello World \^{}

# Beginning and end of line

Common commands:
\end{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

Hello World\textbar{}

\begin{verbatim}

# Vertical movement

This is more complicated.

Suppose:
\end{verbatim}

Line 1: Hello World

Line 2: Hi

Line 1 column 8

# Preferred column

The editor maintains:

cursor column

and:

Move down:

Line 2:

Hi \^{}

\end{verbatim}

Line 3:

abcdefghijk \^{}

\begin{verbatim}

The editor remembers the original intention.

---

# Cursor state

A useful structure:
\end{verbatim}

Cursor

\begin{verbatim}
line

column

preferred_column

\begin{verbatim}

This prevents jumping around while moving vertically.

# 11.6 Scrolling

The document may be larger than the screen.

The editor therefore needs a viewport.

Example:

Document:
\end{verbatim}

Line 1 Line 2 Line 3 \ldots{} Line 1000

\begin{verbatim}

Screen:
\end{verbatim}

Line 500 Line 501 Line 502 Line 503

\begin{verbatim}

The viewport stores:
\end{verbatim}

top visible line = 500

\begin{verbatim}

# Keeping the cursor visible

When the cursor moves:
\end{verbatim}

cursor line

versus

viewport range

\begin{verbatim}

must be checked.

Example:

Screen:
\end{verbatim}

Lines 100-120

\end{verbatim}

Line 121

\begin{verbatim}

New viewport:
\end{verbatim}

Lines 101-121

\begin{verbatim}

# Horizontal scrolling

Long lines create another problem.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

This is a very long line of assembly code\ldots\ldots\ldots\ldots\ldots.
\^{} cursor

\begin{verbatim}

The editor tracks:
\end{verbatim}

left column

\begin{verbatim}

Example:
\end{verbatim}

viewport.left = 40

---

# Redrawing after operations

Every editing operation follows the same pattern:
\end{verbatim}

User input

\begin{verbatim}
|

v

Update document

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Update cursor

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Update viewport

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Render screen

\begin{verbatim}

Do not update the terminal directly from editing code.

The renderer should be the only component that knows how to display.

---

# Testing editing operations

Before combining everything, test each operation.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

buffer\_test

\begin{verbatim}

Tests:
\end{verbatim}

insert character

delete character

split line

join lines

move cursor

scroll viewport

\begin{verbatim}

A small failing test is easier to debug than a broken editor.

---

# The REMEDIT milestone

At the end of this chapter, the editor has the foundations of editing:
\end{verbatim}

Keyboard

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Commands

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Document operations

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Screen rendering

\begin{verbatim}

The editor is no longer just displaying text.

It understands text as data.

That distinction is what separates a terminal display program from a real editor.


# Chapter 12 — Undo and Redo

Undo is one of those features that appears simple from the outside.

The user presses:
\end{verbatim}

Ctrl-Z

\begin{verbatim}

and the previous change disappears.

However, inside the editor, undo is not a display feature.

It is an architectural feature.

A text editor that was not designed with undo in mind usually has to be redesigned to support it properly.

The reason is simple:

The editor must remember what happened.

Not just:
\end{verbatim}

What does the document look like now?

\begin{verbatim}

but:
\end{verbatim}

How did the document get here?

\begin{verbatim}

---

# 12.1 Why undo is an architecture problem

A beginner implementation often looks like this:
\end{verbatim}

keypress

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

modify document

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

display result

\begin{verbatim}

The document changes.

The previous state disappears.

For example:

Before:
\end{verbatim}

Hello

\begin{verbatim}

Insert:
\end{verbatim}

World

\begin{verbatim}

After:
\end{verbatim}

Hello World

\begin{verbatim}

The editor now knows:
\end{verbatim}

Current document:

Hello World

\begin{verbatim}

But undo requires:
\end{verbatim}

What was the previous document?

Hello

\begin{verbatim}

The old information no longer exists.

---

# The naive solution: save the whole file

The simplest undo system is:

Before every change:
\end{verbatim}

copy entire document

\begin{verbatim}

After:
\end{verbatim}

perform operation

\begin{verbatim}

Undo:
\end{verbatim}

restore previous copy

\begin{verbatim}

This works.

It is also wasteful.

Example:

A user edits a 50 MB file.

They type:
\end{verbatim}

a

\begin{verbatim}

The editor copies:
\end{verbatim}

50 MB

\begin{verbatim}

They type another character.

Another:
\end{verbatim}

50 MB

\begin{verbatim}

After a few minutes:

The undo history consumes enormous memory.

---

# The better approach: remember operations

Instead of storing complete document states, store the changes.

The editor already knows:
\end{verbatim}

Insert character Delete character Split line Join line

\begin{verbatim}

Undo is simply performing the opposite operation.

Example:

Normal operation:
\end{verbatim}

Insert `A'

\begin{verbatim}

Undo:
\end{verbatim}

Delete `A'

\begin{verbatim}

Normal operation:
\end{verbatim}

Delete ``hello''

\begin{verbatim}

Undo:
\end{verbatim}

Insert ``hello''

\begin{verbatim}

The editor stores the information required to reverse the action.

---

# Command-based architecture

A useful model is:
\end{verbatim}

User action

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

Command object

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

Execute command

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

Store command history

\begin{verbatim}

Every editing action becomes a command.

Examples:
\end{verbatim}

InsertText

DeleteText

ReplaceText

SplitLine

JoinLine

\begin{verbatim}

The command contains:

- what happened
- where it happened
- enough information to undo it

---

# 12.2 Command records

A command record is a description of one change.

In assembly, this is simply a data structure.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Command

\begin{verbatim}
type

location

data

length
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

The exact layout depends on the REM memory model.

The principle is more important than the implementation.

---

# InsertText

Example:

User types:
\end{verbatim}

A

\end{verbatim}

HelloA

\end{verbatim}

Type:

INSERT\_TEXT

Position:

line = 1 column = 5

Data:

``A''

Delete character at line 1 column 5

\begin{verbatim}

Record:
\end{verbatim}

Position:

line = 1 column = 6

Data:

``World''

Undo:
\end{verbatim}

Insert ``World''

\begin{verbatim}

World

\end{verbatim}

World

\end{verbatim}

1

Before:
\end{verbatim}

Original line boundary

Location

Removed newline

World

# Command execution

A useful design is:
\end{verbatim}

execute(command)

\begin{verbatim}
    |

    v
\end{verbatim}

document changes

\begin{verbatim}
    |

    v

push command onto undo stack

\begin{verbatim}

The command itself knows how to:
\end{verbatim}

apply()

Command

execute()

# 12.3 Undo stack

Undo history is usually implemented as a stack.

A stack follows:
\end{verbatim}

Last In, First Out

LIFO

\begin{verbatim}

Example:

User performs:
\end{verbatim}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Insert A
\item
  Insert B
\item
  Delete C
\end{enumerate}

\begin{verbatim}

History:
\end{verbatim}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 0\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1944}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Delete C \\
Insert B \\
Insert A \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{verbatim}

Undo:

Remove:
\end{verbatim}

Delete C

\begin{verbatim}

Apply reverse:
\end{verbatim}

Insert C

\begin{verbatim}

Stack becomes:
\end{verbatim}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 0\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1944}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Insert B \\
Insert A \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Multiple undo operations

The user presses undo repeatedly.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Undo

Undo

Undo

\begin{verbatim}

The editor walks backwards through history:
\end{verbatim}

Current state

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Command 3 reversed

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Command 2 reversed

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Command 1 reversed

\begin{verbatim}

---

# How many commands?

The editor needs a policy.

Options:

## Unlimited

Advantages:

- simple conceptually

Disadvantages:

- memory grows forever

---

## Fixed number

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Keep last 1000 commands

\begin{verbatim}

Advantages:

- predictable memory

Disadvantages:

- old history disappears

---

## Fixed memory size

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Keep 64 MB of undo data

\begin{verbatim}

Advantages:

- better resource control

Disadvantages:

- more complex

---

For REMEDIT version 1:

A fixed command count or memory limit is probably sufficient.

---

# 12.4 Redo rules

Redo is often misunderstood.

Undo moves backwards.

Redo moves forwards again.

The editor therefore needs a second history.

Normally:
\end{verbatim}

Undo stack

Redo stack

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Example

User types:
\end{verbatim}

A B C

\begin{verbatim}

History:
\end{verbatim}

Undo:

C B A

\begin{verbatim}

User presses undo:
\end{verbatim}

Remove C

\begin{verbatim}

Now:
\end{verbatim}

Undo:

B A

Redo:

C

\begin{verbatim}

Press redo:
\end{verbatim}

Restore C

\begin{verbatim}

---

# The important rule

A new edit destroys the redo history.

Example:

Start:
\end{verbatim}

ABC

\begin{verbatim}

Undo:
\end{verbatim}

AB

\begin{verbatim}

Now type:
\end{verbatim}

D

\begin{verbatim}

Result:
\end{verbatim}

ABD

\begin{verbatim}

The old future:
\end{verbatim}

ABC

\begin{verbatim}

is no longer valid.

The redo stack is cleared.

This rule simplifies the model.

---

# Grouping commands

One usability improvement:

Not every keystroke should necessarily create a separate undo item.

Example:

User types:
\end{verbatim}

hello

\begin{verbatim}

Should undo remove:
\end{verbatim}

h

\begin{verbatim}

or:
\end{verbatim}

hello

\begin{verbatim}

Most editors group continuous typing.

This requires a little more design.

A simple first version can treat each insertion as separate.

A later version can add grouping.

---

# 12.5 Memory management

Undo requires careful memory management.

Every command may contain data.

Example:

Delete:
\end{verbatim}

Delete 5000 characters

\begin{verbatim}

The undo record must store:
\end{verbatim}

5000 characters

\begin{verbatim}

Otherwise the deletion cannot be reversed.

---

# Ownership rules

Every undo record must define:

Who owns this memory?

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Command created

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

copies deleted text

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

undo stack owns copy

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

command removed

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

free memory

\begin{verbatim}

Without clear ownership rules, undo becomes a source of memory leaks.

---

# Simple assembly design

A command record might contain:
\end{verbatim}

Command:

type

line

column

length

data pointer

next pointer

\begin{verbatim}

The stack:
\end{verbatim}

UndoStack

top -\textgreater{} Command \textbar{} v Command \textbar{} v Command

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Testing undo

Undo should be tested separately.

Do not test only through the editor.

Create:
\end{verbatim}

undo\_test

\begin{verbatim}

Test:
\end{verbatim}

insert

undo

check document

delete

undo

check document

replace

undo

check document

\begin{verbatim}

The key question:

> After any sequence of operations, can the editor return to the previous state?

---

# The REMEDIT milestone

After implementing undo and redo:

The editor now has a history.

The architecture becomes:
\end{verbatim}

Keyboard

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Command

\begin{verbatim}
|

+------------+
|            |
v            v
\end{verbatim}

Document Undo history

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Renderer

\begin{verbatim}

Undo is not an optional extra feature.

It affects the design of the entire editor.

A good editor is not only a program that changes text.

It is a program that understands how text changed.


# Part V — Making It a REM Development Environment

> **[LEGACY / PSEUDOCODE]** Some historical examples in this part use the
> original `MOV`, `LOAD`, `STORE`, `JMP`, `CALL`, and `RET` vocabulary. They
> explain earlier REM stages and are not current MyEmulator2 GAS input. Use
> the lowercase forms in Part VIII and Appendix L for buildable code.

# Chapter 13 — File Handling

A text editor without file handling is only a demonstration.

Displaying text proves that the terminal works.

Editing text proves that the data structures work.

Saving and loading proves that the application can become part of a real development environment.

File handling appears simple:
\end{verbatim}

open file

read data

modify data

save file

\begin{verbatim}

However, a reliable editor must consider:

- file formats
- memory allocation
- permissions
- partial writes
- failures
- recovery after crashes

The filesystem is another boundary.

The editor should not care how the storage device works.

It should only care about the file interface provided by Linux.

The basic model is:
\end{verbatim}

REMEDIT

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

libc / syscalls

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Linux filesystem

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Storage

\begin{verbatim}

---

# 13.1 Loading files

Opening an existing file is the first real interaction between REMEDIT and the outside world.

The operation is:
\end{verbatim}

filename

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

open file

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

read contents

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

create document

\begin{verbatim}

---

# The editor workflow

When the user starts:
\end{verbatim}

remedit program.s

\begin{verbatim}

the editor must:

1. Receive the filename.
2. Open the file.
3. Determine its size.
4. Read the contents.
5. Convert the contents into the internal document format.
6. Display the file.

---

# Opening a file

The operating system represents an open file using a file descriptor.

Conceptually:
\end{verbatim}

filename

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

kernel

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

file descriptor

\begin{verbatim}

The editor does not directly manipulate the disk.

It receives a handle:
\end{verbatim}

fd = 3

\begin{verbatim}

and uses that for later operations.

---

# Reading data

The simplest approach:
\end{verbatim}

open file

read all contents

close file

\begin{verbatim}

For a small editor, this is perfectly reasonable.

Example:

File:
\end{verbatim}

hello.asm

\begin{verbatim}

Contents:
\end{verbatim}

MOV R1,R2 ADD R3,R4

\begin{verbatim}

Memory:
\end{verbatim}

Document

Line 1: MOV R1,R2

Line 2: ADD R3,R4

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Converting file data into the document

The filesystem stores bytes.

The editor stores text structures.

These are not necessarily the same thing.

File:
\end{verbatim}

M O V R 1 , R 2 {[}newline{]} A D D \ldots{}

\begin{verbatim}

Document:
\end{verbatim}

Line array

\begin{verbatim}
|
+-- Line 1
|
+-- Line 2
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

The loading process converts:
\end{verbatim}

byte stream

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

editor document model

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Line endings

One early decision:

How does the editor handle line endings?

Different systems use:

Unix:
\end{verbatim}

LF

0x0A

\begin{verbatim}

Windows:
\end{verbatim}

CR LF

0x0D 0x0A

\begin{verbatim}

A good editor normalises internally.

For example:

When loading:
\end{verbatim}

CR LF

\begin{verbatim}

becomes:
\end{verbatim}

LF

\begin{verbatim}

When saving:

the editor writes the chosen format.

---

# Large files

The simple approach:
\end{verbatim}

load entire file into memory

\begin{verbatim}

works well initially.

Advantages:

- simple
- easy to debug
- fast editing

Disadvantages:

- large files consume memory

Advanced editors may use:

- memory mapping
- lazy loading
- paging

REMEDIT version one does not need this.

The first goal is reliability.

---

# File loading errors

Every file operation can fail.

Examples:

File does not exist:
\end{verbatim}

ENOENT

\begin{verbatim}

Permission denied:
\end{verbatim}

EACCES

\begin{verbatim}

Storage failure:
\end{verbatim}

EIO

\begin{verbatim}

The editor should report:
\end{verbatim}

Cannot open file: reason

\begin{verbatim}

not simply:
\end{verbatim}

Error

\begin{verbatim}

Good error messages save debugging time.

---

# 13.2 Saving files

Saving appears easier than loading.

The naive approach:
\end{verbatim}

open file

write contents

close file

\begin{verbatim}

However, saving is where reliability matters most.

The user has changed their work.

A failed save can destroy hours of effort.

---

# The dangerous approach

Consider:
\end{verbatim}

Open original file

Write new contents

Something fails

\begin{verbatim}

The result may be:
\end{verbatim}

Original file corrupted

\begin{verbatim}

The editor has destroyed the only copy.

Never make the original file the first thing you overwrite.

---

# Safer saving

A common approach:
\end{verbatim}

Create temporary file

Write new contents

Flush data

Rename temporary file

\begin{verbatim}

The process:
\end{verbatim}

document

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

temporary file

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

rename

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

original filename

\begin{verbatim}

The rename operation is normally atomic.

The user sees either:

Old version

or:

New version

but not a half-written file.

---

# Save operation

Conceptually:
\end{verbatim}

save()

\begin{verbatim}
create temp file

write document

close file

rename temp -> original

update editor state
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Tracking changes

The editor should know whether the file has changed.

State:
\end{verbatim}

modified = false

\begin{verbatim}

After editing:
\end{verbatim}

modified = true

\begin{verbatim}

After successful save:
\end{verbatim}

modified = false

\begin{verbatim}

The UI can display:
\end{verbatim}

program.s *

\begin{verbatim}

to indicate unsaved changes.

---

# 13.3 Temporary files

Temporary files support safe operations.

They are also useful for:

- recovery
- backups
- intermediate builds

---

# Temporary filename

Example:

Original:
\end{verbatim}

main.s

\begin{verbatim}

Temporary:
\end{verbatim}

.main.s.tmp

\begin{verbatim}

or:
\end{verbatim}

main.s.REMEDIT.tmp

\begin{verbatim}

The important requirement:

Do not accidentally overwrite another file.

---

# Temporary file ownership

The editor must know:

Who owns the temporary file?

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Create temp file

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Editor owns it

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Successful save

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Rename and remove temporary state

\begin{verbatim}

If the editor exits unexpectedly:

the temporary file may still exist.

That can become useful for recovery.

---

# Atomic rename

The key idea:

Writing:
\end{verbatim}

large new file

\begin{verbatim}

directly over:
\end{verbatim}

existing file

\begin{verbatim}

is risky.

Instead:
\end{verbatim}

write complete new version

then switch names

\begin{verbatim}

The rename is the point where the change becomes visible.

This technique is used by many reliable systems.

---

# 13.4 Crash recovery

A serious editor assumes crashes happen.

Possible causes:

- program bug
- power failure
- operating system problem
- hardware failure

The goal is not preventing every crash.

The goal is reducing lost work.

---

# Recovery files

Many editors periodically save recovery information.

Example:

Working file:
\end{verbatim}

main.s

\begin{verbatim}

Recovery file:
\end{verbatim}

.main.s.recovery

\begin{verbatim}

The recovery file contains enough information to restore:

- document contents
- cursor position
- undo information

---

# Simple recovery strategy

For REMEDIT version one:

A practical approach:
\end{verbatim}

When file changes:

\begin{verbatim}
periodically write recovery copy
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

Example:

Every:
\end{verbatim}

5 minutes

\begin{verbatim}

or after:
\end{verbatim}

100 changes

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Recovery workflow

Startup:
\end{verbatim}

Open main.s

Check:

Does recovery file exist?

\begin{verbatim}

If yes:
\end{verbatim}

main.s was modified previously.

Recover?

\begin{verbatim}

The user decides.

---

# Recovery versus autosave

These are different.

Autosave:
\end{verbatim}

replace original file automatically

\begin{verbatim}

Recovery:
\end{verbatim}

preserve emergency copy

\begin{verbatim}

For a development tool, recovery is safer.

The programmer should remain in control of source files.

---

# File module design

The editor should not scatter file operations everywhere.

Create a file module:
\end{verbatim}

file.s

\begin{verbatim}

Responsibilities:
\end{verbatim}

file\_open()

file\_load()

file\_save()

file\_save\_temp()

file\_recover()

\begin{verbatim}

The editor calls:
\end{verbatim}

save document

\begin{verbatim}

It does not need to know:

- filenames
- file descriptors
- temporary naming
- filesystem details

---

# Testing file handling

Before connecting to the editor:

Create:
\end{verbatim}

file\_test

\begin{verbatim}

Tests:
\end{verbatim}

create file

write data

read data

compare contents

rename file

delete file

\begin{verbatim}

Then test failure cases:
\end{verbatim}

missing file

read-only file

invalid path

full storage

\begin{verbatim}

---

# The REMEDIT milestone

After this chapter, the editor has crossed an important boundary.

It is no longer only a memory editor.

It is a development tool.

The architecture now looks like:
\end{verbatim}

Keyboard

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Editor commands

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Document

\begin{verbatim}
|

+------------+

|            |

v            v
\end{verbatim}

Renderer File system

\begin{verbatim}
              |

              v

           Storage
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

A real programmer does not only edit text.

They create files, modify files, build software and recover from mistakes.

Reliable file handling is what turns REMEDIT from an experiment into a tool.


# Chapter 14 — Search and Replace

Search is one of the first features that turns an editor from a text viewer into a useful development tool.

A programmer working on REM will spend a large amount of time searching for:

- instruction names
- labels
- function names
- error messages
- comments
- constants
- configuration values

A good search system is not complicated because finding text is difficult.

Finding text is easy.

The complexity comes from integrating search with:

- the document model
- cursor movement
- selection
- replacement
- undo history

The search system should follow the same architectural principle used throughout REMEDIT:
\end{verbatim}

Search logic

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Document model

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Renderer

\begin{verbatim}

Search should not directly manipulate the screen.

It should operate on the document.

---

# 14.1 Literal search

The first search implementation should be literal text matching.

Given:

Document:
\end{verbatim}

MOV R1,R2 ADD R3,R4 MOV R5,R6

\begin{verbatim}

Search:
\end{verbatim}

MOV

\begin{verbatim}

Results:
\end{verbatim}

Line 1, column 1

Line 3, column 1

\begin{verbatim}

No interpretation.

No regular expressions.

No language awareness.

Just:
\end{verbatim}

Does this sequence of characters exist?

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Search operation

A simple search flow:
\end{verbatim}

User enters search text

\begin{verbatim}
    |

    v
\end{verbatim}

Search module scans document

\begin{verbatim}
    |

    v
\end{verbatim}

Match found

\begin{verbatim}
    |

    v
\end{verbatim}

Move cursor

\begin{verbatim}
    |

    v
\end{verbatim}

Update viewport

\begin{verbatim}
    |

    v
\end{verbatim}

Render

\begin{verbatim}

The search module does not draw anything.

It only reports:
\end{verbatim}

match location

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Searching a line

The simplest case is searching within one line.

Example:

Line:
\end{verbatim}

LOAD R1,VALUE

\begin{verbatim}

Search:
\end{verbatim}

VALUE

\begin{verbatim}

Algorithm:
\end{verbatim}

Start at beginning of line

Compare characters

Move forward

Stop when:

\begin{verbatim}
match found
\end{verbatim}

or:

\begin{verbatim}
end of line reached
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

This is a straightforward character comparison.

---

# Searching the document

A document contains multiple lines.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Line 1 Line 2 Line 3

\begin{verbatim}

The search process:
\end{verbatim}

Search line 1

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Search line 2

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Search line 3

\begin{verbatim}

The result contains:
\end{verbatim}

line number

column number

\begin{verbatim}

The cursor can then move there.

---

# Search direction

Most editors support:

Forward search:
\end{verbatim}

cursor ---\textgreater{} end of file

\begin{verbatim}

Backward search:
\end{verbatim}

cursor \textless--- beginning of file

\begin{verbatim}

Forward search is simpler initially.

The search starts at:
\end{verbatim}

current cursor position

\begin{verbatim}

and continues until:
\end{verbatim}

end of document

\begin{verbatim}

If nothing is found:
\end{verbatim}

Not found

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Wrapping search

Many editors wrap around.

Example:

Cursor:
\end{verbatim}

Line 90

\begin{verbatim}

Search reaches:
\end{verbatim}

end of file

\begin{verbatim}

Instead of failing:
\end{verbatim}

search from beginning

\begin{verbatim}

This requires a decision.

Should REMEDIT:
\end{verbatim}

wrap automatically

\begin{verbatim}

or:
\end{verbatim}

ask the user

\begin{verbatim}

For a first version, either is acceptable.

The important thing is consistency.

---

# Search state

The editor should remember the last search.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

SearchState

\begin{verbatim}
text

direction

case_mode

current_position
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

This allows:
\end{verbatim}

Find next

\begin{verbatim}

without asking for the search text again.

---

# 14.2 Case handling

Case sensitivity is a design choice.

Consider:
\end{verbatim}

MOV

\begin{verbatim}

and:
\end{verbatim}

mov

\begin{verbatim}

Are they the same?

For assembly source, perhaps yes.

For programming languages with case-sensitive identifiers, perhaps no.

---

# Case-sensitive search

The comparison is exact.

Example:

Search:
\end{verbatim}

REM

\begin{verbatim}

Matches:
\end{verbatim}

REM

\begin{verbatim}

Does not match:
\end{verbatim}

rem

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Case-insensitive search

The comparison normalises characters.

Example:

Search:
\end{verbatim}

mov

\begin{verbatim}

Matches:
\end{verbatim}

MOV Mov mov

\begin{verbatim}

The search algorithm compares:
\end{verbatim}

uppercase(character)

\begin{verbatim}

or:
\end{verbatim}

lowercase(character)

\begin{verbatim}

---

# The implementation question

Where should case handling happen?

Two options.

---

## Option 1 — Normalise during comparison

Example:
\end{verbatim}

compare:

uppercase(a)

against:

uppercase(b)

\begin{verbatim}

Advantages:

- document remains unchanged
- simple

Disadvantages:

- slightly more processing

---

## Option 2 — Create normalised search data

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Document:

MOV R1,R2

Search copy:

mov r1,r2

\begin{verbatim}

Advantages:

- faster repeated searching

Disadvantages:

- extra memory
- synchronisation problems

---

For REMEDIT version one:

Normalise during comparison.

The simplicity is worth more than the small performance difference.

---

# 14.3 Replacement

Replacement builds on search.

The operation:
\end{verbatim}

Find text

Replace with different text

\begin{verbatim}

sounds simple.

However, replacement is actually a document modification.

That means it must obey all editor rules:

- update cursor
- update viewport
- create undo history
- maintain document consistency

---

# Single replacement

Example:

Before:
\end{verbatim}

MOV R1,R2

\begin{verbatim}

Search:
\end{verbatim}

R1

\begin{verbatim}

Replace:
\end{verbatim}

R5

\begin{verbatim}

After:
\end{verbatim}

MOV R5,R2

\begin{verbatim}

The operation is:
\end{verbatim}

delete old text

insert new text

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Replace all

Example:

Before:
\end{verbatim}

MOV R1,R2 MOV R1,R3 MOV R1,R4

\begin{verbatim}

Replace:
\end{verbatim}

R1

\begin{verbatim}

with:
\end{verbatim}

R5

\begin{verbatim}

After:
\end{verbatim}

MOV R5,R2 MOV R5,R3 MOV R5,R4

\begin{verbatim}

---

# The danger of replace all

A blind replacement can damage code.

Example:

Search:
\end{verbatim}

A

\begin{verbatim}

Replace:
\end{verbatim}

B

\begin{verbatim}

This may modify:
\end{verbatim}

labels

comments

strings

identifiers

\begin{verbatim}

The editor does not know whether the text has meaning.

A literal replacement does exactly what was requested.

The user must decide whether that is appropriate.

---

# Replacement strategy

A safe implementation:
\end{verbatim}

Find first match

Show location

Ask confirmation

Replace

Continue

\begin{verbatim}

A faster mode:
\end{verbatim}

Replace all

\begin{verbatim}

should clearly indicate:
\end{verbatim}

number of replacements made

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Search and replace in assembly source

REMEDIT will likely be used heavily for assembly.

Useful searches:

Find opcode:
\end{verbatim}

LOAD

\begin{verbatim}

Find register:
\end{verbatim}

R15

\begin{verbatim}

Find label:
\end{verbatim}

main:

\begin{verbatim}

Find syscall:
\end{verbatim}

write

\begin{verbatim}

The editor does not need to understand assembly.

It simply provides reliable text manipulation.

Syntax awareness comes later.

---

# 14.4 Interaction with undo

Search alone does not modify the document.

Replacement does.

Therefore replacement must integrate with undo.

---

# Single replacement

Example:

Before:
\end{verbatim}

Hello World

\begin{verbatim}

Replace:
\end{verbatim}

World

\begin{verbatim}

with:
\end{verbatim}

REM

\begin{verbatim}

Undo record:
\end{verbatim}

Type:

REPLACE\_TEXT

Position:

line 1 column 6

Old:

World

New:

REM

\begin{verbatim}

Undo:
\end{verbatim}

replace REM with World

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Replace all and undo

The question:

Should:
\end{verbatim}

Replace all

\begin{verbatim}

create:
\end{verbatim}

100 undo operations

\begin{verbatim}

or:
\end{verbatim}

1 undo operation

\begin{verbatim}

Most editors choose one operation.

The user expects:
\end{verbatim}

Ctrl-Z

\begin{verbatim}

to undo the entire command.

---

# Grouped undo command

Replace all can create a compound command.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

Command:

REPLACE\_ALL

Contains:

\begin{verbatim}
replacement 1

replacement 2

replacement 3
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

Undo:
\end{verbatim}

reverse all replacements

\begin{verbatim}

This uses the command architecture from the previous chapter.

---

# Memory considerations

Replace all may create many changes.

Example:

A large source tree:
\end{verbatim}

10,000 replacements

\begin{verbatim}

The undo system must store:

- locations
- old text
- new text

A practical solution:

Limit undo memory.

Possible policies:
\end{verbatim}

Keep last N commands

\begin{verbatim}

or:
\end{verbatim}

Keep up to X MB

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Search module design

The editor should isolate search functionality.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

search.s

\begin{verbatim}

Functions:
\end{verbatim}

search\_find()

search\_find\_next()

search\_find\_previous()

replace\_current()

replace\_all()

\begin{verbatim}

The module communicates with:
\end{verbatim}

Document

Cursor

Undo system

\begin{verbatim}

but not directly with:
\end{verbatim}

Terminal

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Testing search and replace

Create:
\end{verbatim}

search\_test

\begin{verbatim}

Test:
\end{verbatim}

find existing text

find missing text

case-sensitive search

case-insensitive search

replace one

replace many

undo replacement

\begin{verbatim}

---

# The REMEDIT milestone

After search and replace:

The editor now has the basic tools needed for real programming work.

A programmer can:
\end{verbatim}

open source

navigate

edit

search

replace

undo mistakes

save

\begin{verbatim}

The next major step is making the editor understand the language being edited.

That is where syntax highlighting begins.


# Chapter 15 — REM Assembly Syntax Highlighting

Syntax highlighting is the first feature where REMEDIT begins to understand the language being edited.

However, an important distinction must be made:

The editor is not an assembler.

It does not:

- validate instructions
- calculate addresses
- resolve symbols
- generate machine code

That remains the responsibility of:
\end{verbatim}

Source code

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

Assembler (GAS)

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

Object code

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

Linker

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

Executable

\begin{verbatim}

The editor only needs enough understanding to help the programmer read and navigate code.

This distinction keeps the design simple.

---

# 15.1 Lexer versus assembler

A common mistake when adding syntax highlighting is attempting to reuse the assembler.

The thought process is:

> "The assembler already understands REM assembly, so the editor should use the assembler."

This is usually the wrong approach.

The assembler has a different purpose.

---

# The assembler

The assembler must answer questions such as:
\end{verbatim}

Is this instruction valid?

Does this register exist?

What machine code represents this instruction?

What address does this label refer to?

\begin{verbatim}

It needs complete knowledge of:

- instruction encoding
- ABI rules
- relocation
- object file format
- symbols

The assembler is a compiler tool.

---

# The lexer

A lexer only asks:
\end{verbatim}

What kind of thing is this text?

\begin{verbatim}

Example:

Input:

MOV R1, 100      ; load value
\end{verbatim}

The lexer identifies:

\begin{verbatim}

MOV
\end{verbatim}

opcode 

R1

\begin{verbatim}
register
\end{verbatim}

100

\begin{verbatim}
number
\end{verbatim}

; load value

\begin{verbatim}
comment
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

It does not decide whether:
\end{verbatim}

MOV R1,100

\begin{verbatim}

is legal.

It only identifies categories.

---

# Why this separation matters

A syntax highlighter should still work while the programmer is writing broken code.

Example:

The user types:

MO
\end{verbatim}

A good editor can already display:

\begin{verbatim}

MO
\end{verbatim}

as probable instruction text.

The assembler cannot help because the instruction is incomplete.

The editor is helping the programmer write code.

It is not compiling code.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{remedit-architecture}{%
\section{REMEDIT architecture}\label{remedit-architecture}}

The flow should be:

\begin{verbatim}

Document
\end{verbatim}

v 

Lexer

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Tokens

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Renderer

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Terminal colours

\begin{verbatim}

The lexer reads text.

The renderer decides how tokens appear.

---

# 15.2 Token types

The first REM assembly lexer does not need to understand everything.

A small number of token types provides most of the value.

Recommended initial types:
\end{verbatim}

opcode

register

number

label

directive

comment

string

identifier

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Opcode

An opcode is an assembly instruction.

Examples:

MOV
ADD
SUB
LOAD
STORE
JMP
CALL
RET
\end{verbatim}

The lexer compares a word against the instruction table.

Example:

Input:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\BuiltInTok{ADD}\NormalTok{ R1}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{R2}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{R3}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Tokens:

\begin{verbatim}

ADD
\end{verbatim}

OPCODE 

R1

\begin{verbatim}
REGISTER
\end{verbatim}

R2

\begin{verbatim}
REGISTER
\end{verbatim}

R3

\begin{verbatim}
REGISTER
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

The renderer can display opcodes in a distinctive colour.

---

# Register

Registers are architecture-specific.

REM already has a defined register set.

Examples:
\end{verbatim}

R0

R1

R15

SP

PC

\begin{verbatim}

The lexer needs a register table:
\end{verbatim}

Register names:

R0

R1

R2

\ldots{}

SP

PC

\begin{verbatim}

When a word matches:
\end{verbatim}

REGISTER

\begin{verbatim}

is emitted.

---

# Number

Assembly contains many numeric forms.

Examples:

Decimal:

100
\end{verbatim}

Hexadecimal:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{0x1000}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Binary:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{0b1010}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Character constants:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{\textquotesingle{}A}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The lexer does not calculate the value.

It only identifies:

\begin{verbatim}

NUMBER
\end{verbatim}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{label}{%
\section{Label}\label{label}}

Labels identify locations in code.

Example:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{loop:}
    \BuiltInTok{ADD}\NormalTok{ R1}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{R2}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{R3}
    \ControlFlowTok{JMP}\NormalTok{ loop}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The lexer sees:

\begin{verbatim}

loop:
\end{verbatim}

and emits:

\begin{verbatim}

LABEL
\end{verbatim}

Important:

The lexer does not know the address.

That happens later during assembly.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{directive}{%
\section{Directive}\label{directive}}

Assembler directives begin with special syntax.

Examples:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{.text}
\NormalTok{.}\BuiltInTok{data}
\NormalTok{.global main}
\NormalTok{.}\DataTypeTok{byte} \DecValTok{10}
\NormalTok{.}\DataTypeTok{word} \DecValTok{100}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The editor can highlight these differently.

The lexer simply recognises:

\begin{verbatim}

DIRECTIVE
\end{verbatim}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{comment}{%
\section{Comment}\label{comment}}

Comments are usually the easiest token.

Example:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\BuiltInTok{ADD}\NormalTok{ R1}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{R2}\OperatorTok{,}\NormalTok{R3    }\CommentTok{; add values}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Everything after:

\begin{verbatim}

;
\end{verbatim}

is:

\begin{verbatim}

COMMENT
\end{verbatim}

The lexer does not inspect the comment contents.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{strings}{%
\section{Strings}\label{strings}}

Strings may appear in assembler data sections.

Example:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{.ascii }\StringTok{"Hello"}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The lexer must recognise:

\begin{verbatim}

"Hello"
\end{verbatim}

as one token.

Otherwise:

\begin{verbatim}

Hello
\end{verbatim}

might incorrectly be treated as an identifier.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{token-representation}{%
\section{Token representation}\label{token-representation}}

The lexer produces records.

Example:

\begin{verbatim}

Token
\end{verbatim}

type

start position

length

text reference 

\begin{verbatim}

Example:

Input:

ADD R1,R2
\end{verbatim}

Tokens:

\begin{verbatim}

TOKEN_OPCODE
\end{verbatim}

ADD 

TOKEN\_REGISTER

\begin{verbatim}
R1
\end{verbatim}

TOKEN\_REGISTER

\begin{verbatim}
R2
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

The renderer uses the token type.

---

# 15.3 State machine design

Like the keyboard decoder, the lexer is easiest to implement as a state machine.

Do not attempt:
\end{verbatim}

search for words

then guess meaning

\begin{verbatim}

Instead process the line from left to right.

---

# Lexer states

A simple assembly lexer:
\end{verbatim}

START

\hfill\break

v

WORD

+----------------+ \textbar{} \textbar{} v v

NUMBER IDENTIFIER

v

COMMENT

\begin{verbatim}

---

# START state

The lexer is looking for the beginning of a token.

Possible characters:

Whitespace:
\end{verbatim}

continue

\begin{verbatim}

Letter:
\end{verbatim}

start word

\begin{verbatim}

Number:
\end{verbatim}

start number

\begin{verbatim}

Comment marker:
\end{verbatim}

start comment

\begin{verbatim}

---

# WORD state

The lexer collects characters.

Example:

Input:
\end{verbatim}

MOV

\begin{verbatim}

Characters:
\end{verbatim}

M O V

\begin{verbatim}

The lexer builds:
\end{verbatim}

MOV

\begin{verbatim}

Then checks:

Is this:
\end{verbatim}

opcode? register? directive? identifier?

\begin{verbatim}

---

# NUMBER state

The lexer collects numeric characters.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

0x1000

\begin{verbatim}

The lexer recognises:
\end{verbatim}

0

x

1

0

0

0

\begin{verbatim}

and produces:
\end{verbatim}

NUMBER

\begin{verbatim}

---

# COMMENT state

Once a comment marker is found:
\end{verbatim}

;

\begin{verbatim}

everything until the end of the line is:
\end{verbatim}

COMMENT

\begin{verbatim}

No further parsing is required.

This is an important optimisation.

---

# First REM lexer implementation

Do not attempt a complete assembler parser.

The first goal:

Make code readable.

Support:
\end{verbatim}

opcode

register

number

label

directive

comment

\begin{verbatim}

That is enough to transform:

.global main

main:
    MOV R1,100
    ADD R2,R1,R3

    ; calculate result
\end{verbatim}

into something much easier to read.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

renderer}\label{integration-with-the-renderer}}

line of text
\end{verbatim}

The lexer produces:

\begin{verbatim}

tokens
\end{verbatim}

The renderer outputs:

\begin{verbatim}

terminal commands
\end{verbatim}

Example:

\begin{verbatim}

Opcode token
\end{verbatim}

v 

Set colour

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Print text

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Performance considerations

Do not re-tokenise the entire file after every keystroke.

A simple first implementation may do this.

It is acceptable.

However, the better model is:
\end{verbatim}

Edit line

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Invalidate line tokens

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Re-tokenise only changed lines

\begin{verbatim}

The document already knows which lines changed.

The lexer can use that information.

---

# Testing the lexer

Create:
\end{verbatim}

lexer\_test

Test input:

.global main

main:
    MOV R1,100      ; load value
\end{verbatim}

Expected:

\begin{verbatim}

DIRECTIVE

LABEL

OPCODE

REGISTER

NUMBER

COMMENT
\end{verbatim}

\hypertarget{the-remedit-milestone}{%
\section{The REMEDIT milestone}\label{the-remedit-milestone}}

After syntax highlighting:

The editor has become aware of the language.

The architecture now looks like:

\begin{verbatim}

Source text
\end{verbatim}

\textbar{}

v 

Document

\begin{verbatim}
 |

 v
\end{verbatim}

Lexer

\begin{verbatim}
 |

 v
\end{verbatim}

Tokens

\begin{verbatim}
 |

 v
\end{verbatim}

Renderer

\begin{verbatim}
 |

 v
\end{verbatim}

Terminal

\begin{verbatim}

The assembler remains responsible for correctness.

The editor is responsible for helping the programmer think.

That is exactly the right division of responsibility.


# Chapter 16 — Building from the Editor

An editor becomes a development environment when it can perform the complete development cycle:
\end{verbatim}

write code

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

build code

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

run or debug code

\begin{verbatim}

For REM, this is especially important.

The entire purpose of building REMEDIT is not just editing text.

It is creating a native environment where REM programs can be developed on REM itself.

The goal:
\end{verbatim}

Edit assembly

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

Press F5

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

Assembler runs

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

Linker runs

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

Executable produced

\begin{verbatim}

The editor becomes the front end to the existing REM toolchain.

---

# 16.1 The F5 workflow

Many editors provide a build shortcut.

Examples:

- Emacs:
\end{verbatim}

M-x compile

\begin{verbatim}

- IDEs:
\end{verbatim}

Build button

\begin{verbatim}

For REMEDIT:
\end{verbatim}

F5 = Build project

\begin{verbatim}

The user should not have to leave the editor.

---

# Basic workflow

The sequence:

**Flow:** Save → clone → execve make → capture output → display errors

Each stage has a purpose.

---

# Step 1 — Save

Before building:

The editor should save the current file.

Why?

Because the compiler works on files.

The compiler does not know about the unsaved text currently held in memory.

Example:

Editor contains:
\end{verbatim}

main.s

MOV R1,100

\begin{verbatim}

Disk contains:
\end{verbatim}

main.s

MOV R1,50

\begin{verbatim}

If the editor runs:
\end{verbatim}

make

\begin{verbatim}

the assembler sees:
\end{verbatim}

MOV R1,50

\begin{verbatim}

not:
\end{verbatim}

MOV R1,100

\begin{verbatim}

The build command must operate on the current document.

---

# Step 2 — clone

A running editor is a process.

The build command is another process.

The editor should not replace itself.

The relationship is:
\end{verbatim}

REMEDIT

\begin{verbatim}
|

| clone()

|

+----------------+

                 |

                 v

             build process
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

The original editor continues running.

The child process prepares to run the build command.

On the current Linux port, this is implemented with the Linux userspace
convention rather than a guessed desktop wrapper: put the syscall number in
`r1`, put arguments in `r2`–`r7`, call the `syscall` instruction, and inspect
the result in `r1`. The process launcher uses `clone` (220), `execve` (221),
and `wait4` (260) in the currently exercised table. `execve` requires an
executable path, an argument vector, and an environment vector. The parent
must decode the `wait4` result and preserve the build exit status.

---

# Why not simply run make directly?

Because:
\end{verbatim}

make

\begin{verbatim}

would replace the editor process.

The editor would disappear.

The user could not return to editing.

The operating system process model exists specifically to allow this.

---

# Step 3 — exec make

After clone:

The child process uses:
\end{verbatim}

execve()

\begin{verbatim}

to replace itself with:
\end{verbatim}

make

\begin{verbatim}

The flow becomes:
\end{verbatim}

Child process

\begin{verbatim}
    |

    v
\end{verbatim}

execve(``/path/to/make'', argv, envp)

\begin{verbatim}
    |

    v
\end{verbatim}

make is now running

\begin{verbatim}

The editor process is unaffected.

---

# The complete process tree

During a build:
\end{verbatim}

Linux kernel

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  +----------------+

  |                |

  v                v
\end{verbatim}

REMEDIT make

\begin{verbatim}
                  |

                  v


              assembler


                  |

                  v


               linker
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

This is exactly how normal Linux development works.

REM is not inventing a special system.

It is providing a normal Unix development environment on a new architecture.

---

# Capturing build output

A build produces text:

Example:

Successful:
\end{verbatim}

as main.s -o main.o

ld main.o -o main

\begin{verbatim}

Failed:
\end{verbatim}

main.s:42: unknown instruction

\begin{verbatim}

The editor needs to capture this output.

---

# 16.2 Pipes

A pipe is a communication channel between processes.

The model:
\end{verbatim}

Process A

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

pipe

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

Process B

\begin{verbatim}

For the build system:
\end{verbatim}

make

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

pipe

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

REMEDIT

\begin{verbatim}

The build output becomes input for the editor.

---

# Standard output

Normally:
\end{verbatim}

make

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

terminal

\begin{verbatim}

The user sees:
\end{verbatim}

assembling\ldots{} linking\ldots{} error

\begin{verbatim}

With a pipe:
\end{verbatim}

make

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

REMEDIT buffer

\begin{verbatim}

The editor can display:
\end{verbatim}

Build Results

main.s:42 error: unknown opcode

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Standard error

Programs normally have two output streams:
\end{verbatim}

stdout

stderr

\begin{verbatim}

stdout:

Normal output.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

assembling main.s

\begin{verbatim}

stderr:

Errors and warnings.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

main.s:42: invalid register

\begin{verbatim}

The editor should capture both.

---

# Pipe design

A simple build pipeline:
\end{verbatim}

Create pipe

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

clone()

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  +----------------+

  |                |

  v                v
\end{verbatim}

child parent

execve make read pipe

\begin{verbatim}

The child redirects:
\end{verbatim}

stdout

stderr

\begin{verbatim}
    |

    v
\end{verbatim}

pipe

\begin{verbatim}

The parent reads:
\end{verbatim}

pipe

\begin{verbatim}
|

v
\end{verbatim}

build output window

---

# Build output window

The editor should not overwrite the source file.

Instead:
\end{verbatim}

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 0\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}

---

# 16.3 Process control

Launching a process is only part of the problem.

The editor also needs to manage it.

---

# Waiting for completion

After starting:
\end{verbatim}

make

\begin{verbatim}

the editor can wait:
\end{verbatim}

waitpid()

\begin{verbatim}

The result:
\end{verbatim}

Build complete

success

\begin{verbatim}

or:
\end{verbatim}

Build failed

exit code 1

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Blocking versus asynchronous builds

Two approaches:

---

## Simple blocking build

The editor:
\end{verbatim}

starts make

waits

shows result

\begin{verbatim}

Advantages:

- simple
- easy to debug

Disadvantages:

- editor freezes during build

For a first REMEDIT version:

This is acceptable.

---

## Asynchronous build

The editor:
\end{verbatim}

starts make

continues editing

receives output later

\begin{verbatim}

Advantages:

- better user experience

Disadvantages:

- more complexity

Requires:

- process tracking
- event handling
- background output management

---

# Build status

The editor should know:
\end{verbatim}

BUILDING

SUCCESS

FAILED

\begin{verbatim}

Example:

Status line:
\end{verbatim}

Building\ldots{}

\begin{verbatim}

then:
\end{verbatim}

Build successful

\begin{verbatim}

or:
\end{verbatim}

Build failed: 3 errors

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Terminating a build

Sometimes:
\end{verbatim}

make

\begin{verbatim}

takes too long.

The user may want:
\end{verbatim}

Ctrl-C

\begin{verbatim}

The editor may need to:

- send a signal
- terminate child processes
- clean up pipes

This is where the earlier terminal signal discussion becomes relevant.

---

# 16.4 Error navigation

A build environment becomes much more useful when errors are clickable.

Example compiler output:
\end{verbatim}

main.s:42: unknown opcode MOVX

\begin{verbatim}

The editor should understand:
\end{verbatim}

filename

line number

message

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Error parser

The editor can scan output.

Input:
\end{verbatim}

Extract:
\end{verbatim}

file:

main.s

line:

42

Then provide:
\end{verbatim}

main.s

# REM development workflow

The final goal:

Open source file

Create:

---

# The REMEDIT milestone

After this chapter:

REMEDIT is no longer just an editor.

It is a development environment.

It can:

- edit source
- understand assembly
- build programs
- display errors
- guide the programmer back to mistakes

This is the point where REM becomes self-hosting in spirit.

The machine is no longer just running software.

It is becoming capable of creating its own software.


# Part VI — Advanced REM Tools

# Chapter 17 — REM System Monitor

> **Status: [PLANNED]** The monitor described here is a design target. The
> existing `reminfo` utility is the narrower implemented diagnostic.

## Current status

The monitor described here is a planned REMEDIT project, except where a
specific diagnostic is identified as existing. The repository's `reminfo`
program reads the interfaces available in a particular Linux rootfs,
including `uname`, uptime, and selected `/proc` files. A disposable test
rootfs may not mount `/proc`, and a userspace monitor cannot read arbitrary
process registers without a kernel or debugger interface. Treat process,
register, and performance-counter displays as capability-dependent features.

A development environment is not complete when it can only create programs.

A programmer also needs tools to understand what those programs are doing.

Traditional Unix systems provide tools such as:
\end{verbatim}

ps

top

vmstat

free

lsof

/proc

\begin{verbatim}

These tools answer questions:

- What programs are running?
- How much memory are they using?
- Are they consuming CPU?
- What resources do they have open?
- What is the system doing internally?

REM should eventually provide similar tools.

The purpose of a system monitor is not only convenience.

It is also a test of the architecture.

A system monitor exercises:

- process management
- memory management
- kernel interfaces
- scheduling
- hardware counters

If REM can support its own monitoring tools, the operating system environment is becoming mature.

---

# 17.1 The system monitor architecture

The system monitor sits above the kernel.

The architecture:
\end{verbatim}

REM Monitor

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

System information interface

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

Linux kernel

\begin{verbatim}
  |

  v
\end{verbatim}

REM CPU

\begin{verbatim}

The monitor should not directly inspect hardware.

It should ask the operating system:

"Tell me the current state."

---

# Information sources

A Unix-like system normally exposes information through:
\end{verbatim}

system calls

/proc filesystem

kernel interfaces

\begin{verbatim}

The exact REM implementation depends on what the kernel provides.

The application should use the highest-level interface available.

---

# 17.2 Process monitoring

A process is a running program.

Examples:
\end{verbatim}

remedit

shell

assembler

linker

\begin{verbatim}

The monitor should display:
\end{verbatim}

PID

name

state

CPU usage

memory usage

\begin{verbatim}

Example:
\end{verbatim}

PID NAME STATE

1 init running

20 shell sleeping

35 remedit running

42 make waiting

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Process identifiers

Every running process has an identifier:
\end{verbatim}

PID

\begin{verbatim}

Example:
\end{verbatim}

PID 35

\begin{verbatim}

The kernel uses this value to identify the process.

Tools such as:
\end{verbatim}

kill

\begin{verbatim}

use the PID:
\end{verbatim}

kill 35

\begin{verbatim}

meaning:

"send a signal to process 35."

---

# Process states

A process is not always running.

Common states:
\end{verbatim}

RUNNING

READY

SLEEPING

WAITING

STOPPED

\begin{verbatim}

Example:

A shell waiting for input:
\end{verbatim}

SLEEPING

\begin{verbatim}

An assembler currently executing:
\end{verbatim}

RUNNING

\begin{verbatim}

Understanding process states helps explain system behaviour.

---

# Process tree

Processes have relationships.

Example:
\end{verbatim}

init

+-- shell

\begin{verbatim}
   |
   +-- remedit

   |
   +-- make

          |
          +-- assembler

          |
          +-- linker
\end{verbatim}

\begin{verbatim}

A useful monitor view:
\end{verbatim}

PID PROCESS

1 init 10 shell 20 remedit 30 make 31 as 32 ld

\begin{verbatim}

This immediately shows how programs are related.

---

# 17.3 Memory monitoring

Memory is one of the most important parts of a new CPU architecture.

The monitor should display:
\end{verbatim}

total memory

used memory

free memory

process memory

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Virtual memory view

Modern Linux programs do not normally use physical addresses directly.

The model is:
\end{verbatim}

Application

\begin{verbatim}
 |

 v
\end{verbatim}

Virtual address

\begin{verbatim}
 |

 v
\end{verbatim}

MMU

\begin{verbatim}
 |

 v
\end{verbatim}

Physical memory

\begin{verbatim}

The monitor should reflect this.

For a process:
\end{verbatim}

Virtual memory:

code

data

heap

stack

\begin{verbatim}

---

# Process memory layout

A typical process:
\end{verbatim}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 0\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2361}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Stack \\
Heap \\
Data \\
Code \\

\begin{verbatim}

The monitor can display:
\end{verbatim}

PID 35 remedit

Code: 128 KB
